What is a Monoid?
Paul Blain Levy, Morgan Rogers

Abstract
In many situations one encounters a notion that resembles that of a monoid. It consists of a carrier and two operations that resemble a unit and a multiplication, subject to three equations that resemble associativity and left and right unital laws. The question then arises whether this notion in fact that of a monoid in a suitable sense. Category theorists have answered this question by providing a notion of monoid in a monoidal category, or more generally in a multicategory. While this encompasses many examples, it is unsuitable in other cases, such as the notion of relative monad, and the modelling of call-by-push-value sequencing. In each of these examples, the leftmost and/or the rightmost factor of a multiplication or associativity law seems to be distinguished. To include such examples, we generalize the multicategorical framework in two stages. Firstly, we move to the framework of a left-skew multicategory (due to Bourke and Lack), which generalizes both multicategory and left-skew monoidal category. The notion of monoid in this framework encompasses examples where only the leftmost factor is distinguished, such as the notion of relative monad. Secondly, we consider monoids in the novel framework of a bi-skew multicategory. This encompasses examples where both the leftmost and the rightmost factor are distinguished, such as the notion of a category on a span, and the modelling of call-by-push-value sequencing. In the bi-skew framework (which is the most general), we give a coherence result saying that a monoid corresponds to an unbiased monoid, i.e. a map from the unit bi-skew multicategory.



Note

An early version of this work
[
1
]
was presented at Category Theory 2019.

I
Introduction

What is a monoid? Traditionally, it is just a set equipped with an associative binary operation and an identity element. But this is not the whole story. For in mathematics and computer science, we often encounter other notions that seem to follow a similar pattern, featuring two
operations
: a binary operation clearly identifiable as the multiplication, and a nullary operation clearly identifiable as the unit. Furthermore, these are required to satisfy three
equational laws
, clearly identifiable as associativity, left identity and right identity.

Here is one example.
1
1

1

We use the traditional set/class distinction. More on that later.
Fix a class
E
—we may call its elements
objects
. A
category on
E

consists a family of sets
(\mathcal{C}(x,y))_{x,y\in E}
, whose elements we call
morphisms
, together with a composition function

;_{x,y,z}:\mathcal{C}(x,y)\times\mathcal{C}(y,z)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(x,z)

for all
x,y,z\!\in\!E
and an identity element
\mathsf{id}_{x}\!\in\!\mathcal{C}(x,x)
for all
x\!\in\!E
, satisfying the usual associativity and identity laws. (From here on, we omit the subscript
x
when it is clear to which object an identity morphism is attached.)

In order to encompass such notions, researchers generalized the traditional notion of monoid to that of
monoid in a monoidal category
or—more general still—that of
monoid in a multicategory
. This has proved to be a successful abstract framework, encompassing such important notions as ring, monad on a given category, and category on a given class.

However, for certain purposes the framework is not general enough. That is, there are notions that do not fit this framework but still seem to follow the
“monoid”
pattern. We give several examples.

1.

A
class span
consists of classes
A,B,E
and functions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

Say that a
category
on this class span consists of a family of sets
(\mathcal{C}(a,b))_{a\in A,b\in B}
, together with a composition function
;_{a,i,b}:\mathcal{C}(a,gi)\times\mathcal{C}(fi,b)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(a,b)
for all
a\!\in\!A,i\!\in\!E,b\!\in\!B
, and an identity
\mathsf{id}_{i}\!\in\!\mathcal{C}(fi,gi)
for all
i\!\in\!E
, satisfying the laws in Figure
1
. This seems to follow the monoid pattern.

2.

Recall the
call-by-push-value
(CBPV) calculus
[
2
]
.
It distinguishes between values
\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{v}}V:A
, which have value type, and computations
\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{c}}M:\underline{B}
, which have computation type. In particular,
FA
is the type of computations that aim to return a value of type
A
. The rules and laws for
F
are shown in Figure
2
. This seem to follow the pattern described:
\mathtt{return}
serves as a unit, sequencing (
\mathtt{to}
) a composition, and it is clear how to label the three laws as left identity, right identity and associativity. So a model of the
F
fragment of CBPV should be describable as a monoid in some sense.

3.

Two notions of
relative monad
has appeared in the literature: a
“forward”
notion in the work of Altenkirch
et al.

[
3
]
, and a
“backward”
notion in the work of Spivey
[
4
]
. Each of them seems to follow the monoid pattern.

•

For any
i\!\in\!E
and
b\!\in\!B
and
l:fi\rightarrow b
, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{id}_{i};l

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle l

•

For any
a\!\in\!A
and
i\!\in\!E
and
h:a\rightarrow gi
, we have

\displaystyle h;\mathsf{id}_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h

•

For any
a\!\in\!A
and
i,j\!\in\!E
and
b\!\in\!B
,

and
h:a\rightarrow gi
and
k:fi\rightarrow gj
and
l:fj\rightarrow b
,

we have

\displaystyle(h;k);l

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h;(k;l)

Laws of a category on the class span
(A,B,E,f,g)

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 24.60126pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{v}}V:A$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=91.95792pt\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{c}}\mathtt{return}\ V:FA\ $}}}}\\
\\
{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{c}}M:FA\quad\Gamma,x:A\vdash^{\mathsf{c}}N:\underline{B}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=127.61765pt\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 22.2013pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{c}}M\ \mathtt{to}\ x.\ N:\underline{B}$}}}}\\
\\
{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 28.84778pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{v}}V:A\quad\Gamma,x:A\vdash^{\mathsf{c}}N:\underline{B}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=174.87431pt\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{c}}(\mathtt{return}\ V)\ \mathtt{to}\ x.\ N=N[V/x]:\underline{B}$}}}}\\
\\
{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 50.16643pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{c}}M:FA$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=153.52715pt\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{c}}M\ \mathtt{to}\ x.\ \mathtt{return}\ x=M:FA$}}}}\\
\\
{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 17.08398pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{c}}M:FA\quad\Gamma,x:A\vdash^{\mathsf{c}}N:FB\quad\Gamma,y:B\vdash^{\mathsf{c}}P:\underline{C}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=248.71011pt\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{c}}(M\ \mathtt{to}\ x.\ N)\ \mathtt{to}\ y.\ P=M\ \mathtt{to}\ x.\ (N\ \mathtt{to}\ y.\ P):\underline{C}$}}}}\end{array}

Fig. 2:
Typing rules and laws for
F
in call-by-push-value.

Conventions: the underline in
\underline{B}
indicates a computation type, and a typing context
\Gamma
is a list of declarations
x:A
that assign a value type to an identifier.

The goal of this paper is to generalize the multicategorical framework so as to encompass all of these notions. This is done in two stages. The first uses the notion of
left-skew multicategory

[
5
]
. As we shall see, the resulting framework encompasses both notions of relative monad. There is also a dual notion of
right-skew multicategory
.

The second stage introduces the notion of
bi-skew multicategory
, which generalizes both left-skew and right-skew multicategory. The progressive generalizations are depicted in Figure
3
. As we shall see, the resulting framework encompasses the notions of category on a class span, and model of the
F
fragment of CBPV.

Note that, in the associativity law of Figure
1
, the leftmost and rightmost premises are special. Each premise except the leftmost is a morphism from an
f
object, and each premise except the rightmost is a morphism to a
g
object. Likewise in Figure
2
, each premise except the leftmost has an extended context, and each premise except the rightmost has an
F
type. As we shall see, this reflects the essential difference between traditional multicategories and their skew variants; the latter allow various kinds of
“tight”
morphisms with a special leftmost and/or rightmost input.

Structure of paper

The paper is structured as follows. After recalling some background concepts in Section
II
, we recall the setting of (ordinary) multicategories in Section
III
and some familiar examples of monoids in multicategories; many familiar examples are derived from the special case of
representable
multicategories, which are those corresponding to monoidal categories. Section
IV
treats a step up in generality, left-skew multicategories, justified in Section
IV-A
by an examination of relative monads. Section
V
takes us to our final level of generality with bi-skew multicategories, motivated this time by an examination of CBPV in Section
V-A
.

Having accomplished the main goal of the paper, we proceed to consider the notion of
unbiased
monoid, which includes an
n
-ary operation for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
. In Section
VI
, we formulate this in the multicategorical setting and indeed to the skew and bi-skew settings. Happily, in each case, it is equivalent to our notion of monoid.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{l}\text{Monoidal categories}\\
\text{and lax maps}\end{array}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{l}\text{Left skew-monoidal}\\
\text{categories, lax maps}\end{array}}
Multicategories
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{l}\text{Right skew-monoidal}\\
\text{categories, lax maps}\end{array}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{l}\text{Left skew}\\
\text{multicategories}\end{array}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{l}\text{Right skew}\\
\text{multicategories}\end{array}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{array}[]{l}\text{Bi-skew}\\
\text{multicategories}\end{array}}

Fig. 3:
2-categories and 2-functors

II
Preliminaries

II-A

Sets, classes and collections

In this paper, we adopt the ontology of
[
6
]
.
This means, firstly, that we distinguish the notions of set and class; in particular, we have the class
\mathsf{Set}
of all sets. But we also have a more general notion of collection; in particular, we have the collection of all classes, and the collection of all endofunctions on
\mathsf{Set}
.
2
2

2

The word “conglomerate” is used in
[
6
]
.

This leads to two notions of category:

•

A
light category

\mathcal{C}
consists of a class
|{\mathcal{C}}|
and hom-set
\mathcal{C}(x,y)
for all
x,y\in|{\mathcal{C}}|
, with composition and identities. For example, the category
\mathbf{Set}
of all sets.

•

More generally, a
hyperlarge category

\mathcal{C}
consists of a collection
|{\mathcal{C}}|
and hom-collection
\mathcal{C}(x,y)
for all
x,y\in|{\mathcal{C}}|
, with composition and identities. For example, the category of all classes, and the category of all endofunctors on
\mathbf{Set}
.

A hyperlarge category
\mathcal{C}
is
moderate
when the object collection and hom-collections are all classes,
small
when they are all sets, and
locally small
when the hom-collections are all sets. Thus light is the conjunction of moderate and locally small.

The adjectives “light” and “hyperlarge” will usually be omitted. For various categorical notions, such as multicategory, we define only the light version. The hyperlarge version is then obtained by replacing “class” and “set” with “collection”.

II-B

Bimodules

Definition 1
.

For (light) categories
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
, a
bimodule
3
3

3

Also known as
profunctors
or
distributors
(for a dual variance convention).

\mathcal{O}:\mathcal{C}\,\to\hskip-6.49994pt\shortmid\hskip 5.0pt\mathcal{D}
consists of the following data.

•

For each
\mathcal{C}
-object
x
and
\mathcal{D}
-object
y
, a set
\mathcal{O}(x,y)
, called the “hom-set” from
x
to
y
. An element
g\!\in\!\mathcal{O}(x,y)
is called an
\mathcal{O}
-morphism
and written
g:x\rightarrow y
.

•

For any
\mathcal{C}
-objects
x,y
and
\mathcal{D}
-object
z
and
\mathcal{C}
-morphism
f:x\rightarrow y
and
\mathcal{O}
-morphism
g:y\rightarrow z
, the
composite

\mathcal{O}
-morphism
f;g:x\rightarrow z
.

•

For any
\mathcal{C}
-object
x
and
\mathcal{D}
-objects
y,z
and
\mathcal{O}
-morphism
g:x\rightarrow y
and
\mathcal{D}
-morphism
h:y\rightarrow z
, the
composite

\mathcal{O}
-morphism
g;h:x\rightarrow z
.

The following equations must be satisfied for any
\mathcal{O}
-morphism
g:x\rightarrow y
:

•

Left and right unitality:

\mathsf{id}_{x};g=g=g;\mathsf{id}_{y}

•

Associativity:
for
f^{\prime}:x^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow x^{\prime}
and
f:x^{\prime}\rightarrow x
in
\mathcal{C}
and
h:y\rightarrow y^{\prime}
and
h^{\prime}:y^{\prime}\rightarrow y^{\prime\prime}
in
\mathcal{D}
we have,

\displaystyle(f^{\prime};f);g

\displaystyle=f^{\prime};(f;g)

\displaystyle(f;g);h

\displaystyle=f;(g;h)

\displaystyle g;(h;h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(g;h);h^{\prime}

A bimodule
\mathcal{C}\,\to\hskip-6.49994pt\shortmid\hskip 5.0pt\mathcal{D}
can be expressed in curried form as a functor
\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathbf{Set}
. However, this observation does not adapt to the setting of hyperlarge bimodules between hyperlarge categories, as there is no “category of all collections” to replace
\mathbf{Set}
.

Definition 2
.

We provide two ways of constructing a bimodule from a functor:

1.

A functor
F:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
yields a bimodule
\mathsf{Forward}_{F}:\mathcal{C}\,\to\hskip-6.49994pt\shortmid\hskip 5.0pt\mathcal{D}
via
\mathsf{Forward}_{F}(x,y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\rm{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}\mathcal{D}(Fx,y)
.

2.

A functor
U:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
yields a bimodule
\mathsf{Backward}_{U}:\mathcal{C}\,\to\hskip-6.49994pt\shortmid\hskip 5.0pt\mathcal{D}
via
\mathsf{Backward}_{U}(x,y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\rm{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}\mathcal{C}(x,Uy)
.

Note that the construction
U\mapsto\mathsf{Backward}_{U}
is contravariant at the 1-level, whereas the construction
F\mapsto\mathsf{Forward}_{F}
is contravariant at the 2-level.

Example 1
.

An adjunction
F\dashv U:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
consists of functors
U:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
and
F:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
, together with a bimodule isomorphism
\mathsf{Forward}_{F}\cong\mathsf{Backward}_{U}
.

We sometimes speak of a bimodule
\mathcal{O}
from a category to a class, or from a class to a category. In these cases, an
\mathcal{O}
-morphism can be composed at one end only.

We can also speak of one-sided modules: for a category
\mathcal{C}
, a
left
\mathcal{C}
-module
provides for each
\mathcal{C}
-object
x
a set
\mathcal{N}(x)
called the
“hom-set”
from
x
. An element
g\!\in\!\mathcal{N}(x)
is written
g:x\rightarrow
and can be composed with a morphism
x^{\prime}\rightarrow x
in a way that satisfies the associativity and left-identity laws. Such a module can be expressed in curried form as a functor
\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{op}}\rightarrow\mathbf{Set}
.

II-C

Monoidal and skew monoidal categories

As further motivation, we recall a generalization of monoidal categories called
left-skew monoidal categories
. These were originally introduced by Szlachányi in
[
7
]
and then developed by Street and Lack in a series of papers including
[
8
]
. More recently, Uustalu, Veltri and Zeilberger proposed a sequent calculus for left-skew monoidal categories
[
9
]
.

Definition 3
.

A
left-skew monoidal category
consists of a category
\mathcal{C}
, a bifunctor
\otimes:\mathcal{C}^{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
, an object
I
and natural transformations

\begin{array}[]{clrcl}\alpha_{x,y,z}&:&(x\otimes y)\otimes z&\rightarrow&x\otimes(y\otimes z)\\
\lambda_{x}&:&I\otimes x&\rightarrow&x\\
\rho_{x}&:&x&\rightarrow&x\otimes I\end{array}

such that the following diagrams commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I\otimes I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(x\otimes I)\otimes y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x\otimes(I\otimes y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x\otimes y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x\otimes y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{x,I,y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x\otimes\lambda_{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{x}\otimes y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(I\otimes x)\otimes y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{I\otimes(x\otimes y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(x\otimes y)\otimes I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x\otimes(y\otimes I)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x\otimes y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x\otimes y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{x,y,I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{x}\otimes y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{x\otimes y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{x,I,y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{x\otimes y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x\otimes\rho_{y}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(x\otimes(y\otimes z))\otimes w}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x\otimes((y\otimes z)\otimes w)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{((x\otimes y)\otimes z)\otimes w}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x\otimes(y\otimes(z\otimes w))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(x\otimes y)\otimes(z\otimes w)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{x,y\otimes z,w}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x\otimes\alpha_{y,z,w}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{x,y,z}\otimes w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{x\otimes y,z,w}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{x,y,z\otimes w}}

Given left-skew monoidal categories
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
, a
lax map

\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
is a functor
F
together with a morphism
\varepsilon:I\rightarrow FI
and natural transformation
\mu_{x,y}:Fx\otimes Fy\rightarrow F(x\otimes y)
natural in
x
and
y
, compatible with the structure. A
monoidal natural transformation

\alpha:(F,\varepsilon,\mu)\rightarrow(F^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
is a natural transformation compatible with the structure. Thus we obtain a 2-category of left-skew monoidal categories.

Given a left-skew monoidal category, we write e.g.
\bigotimes_{x}(a,b,c)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\rm{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}((x\otimes a)\otimes b)\otimes c
. To be precise,
\bigotimes_{x}
recursively sends the empty list to
x
and a list
\vec{a},y
to
(\bigotimes_{x}\vec{a})\otimes y
. We abbreviate
\bigotimes_{I}
as
\bigotimes
.

A
monoidal category
is a left-skew monoidal category where
\alpha,\rho,\lambda
are all isomorphisms. In this case, the list of coherence requirements can be reduced.

III
Basic level of abstraction: multicategory

III-A

The 2-category of multicategories

We give the notion of multicategory, followed later by its variants. We formulate these notions using composition at a single input; some authors prefer an alternative formulation using simultaneous composition.

Definition 4
.

A
multicategory

\mathcal{C}
consists of the following data.

•

A class
|{\mathcal{C}}|
of objects.

•

For any object list
\vec{a}=(a_{0},\ldots,a_{n-1})
and object
x
, a set of morphisms
\mathcal{C}(\vec{a};x)
. Such a
\mathcal{C}
-morphism is written
f:\vec{a}\rightarrow x
or pictorially as:

Note that the height of these morphisms is arbitrary. We use a double-headed arrow over the name of a list (for example,
\accentset{\twoheadrightarrow}{a}
) to indicate that it is assumed non-empty.

Strictly speaking, this indexing in the composition symbol is ambiguous, since
x
may appear several times in the domain. We rely on positioning in the diagrams to resolve this ambiguity.

We obtain a 2-category of multicategories as follows.

Definition 5
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
be multicategories.

1.

A
map

F:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
sends each
\mathcal{C}
-object to a
\mathcal{D}
-object, and each
\mathcal{C}
-morphism
f:a_{0},\ldots,a_{n-1}\rightarrow b
to a
\mathcal{D}
-morphism
Ff:Fa_{0},\ldots,Fa_{n-1}\rightarrow Fb
in a way that preserves identities and composition.

2.

Given maps
F,G:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
, a
2-cell

\alpha:F\Rightarrow G
sends each
\mathcal{C}
-object
x
to a
\mathcal{D}
-morphism
\alpha_{x}:Fx\rightarrow Gx
that is natural in the sense that, for any

The
2
-category of multicategories is denoted
\mathsf{Mult}
. It has all (set-indexed) products; in particular it has a terminal object, denoted
1
.

3.

We write
\mathbf{Mon}({\mathcal{C}})
for the category of monoids and homomorphisms in
\mathcal{C}
.

Here are some examples.

1.

Write
\mathbf{Set}
for the multicategory of sets, where a morphism
A_{0},\ldots,A_{n-1}\rightarrow B
is simply a function
A_{0}\times\cdots\times A_{n-1}\rightarrow B
. Then a monoid in
\mathbf{Set}
is just a monoid in the traditional sense.

2.

Write
\mathbf{Ab}
for the multicategory of abelian groups
(X,+,0)
, where a morphism
A_{0},\ldots,A_{n-1}\rightarrow B
is a function
A_{0}\times\cdots\times A_{n-1}\rightarrow B
that preserves
+
and
0
in each argument. Then a monoid in
\mathbf{Ab}
is just a ring.

3.

A
complete lattice
is a poset where every subset has a supremum (or equivalently, every subset has an infimum). Write
\mathbf{SupLat}
for the multicategory of complete lattices, where a morphism
A_{0},\ldots,A_{n-1}\rightarrow B
is a function
A_{0}\times\cdots\times A_{n-1}\rightarrow B
preserving suprema in each argument. A monoid in
\mathbf{SupLat}
is a
quantale
.

4.

A
set-valued matrix

X:A\,\to\hskip-6.49994pt\shortmid\hskip 5.0ptB
, for classes
A
and
B
, is a family of sets
(X(a,b))_{a\in A,b\in B}
. For a class
E
, write
\mathbf{SetMat}_{E}
for the multicategory of set-valued endomatrices on
E
, where a morphism
X_{0},\ldots,X_{n-1}
to
Y
is a function sending each list
a_{0},\ldots,a_{n}\!\in\!E
and list
x_{0}\!\in\!X_{0}(a_{0},a_{1}),\ldots,x_{n-1}\!\in\!X_{n-1}(a_{n-1},a_{n})
to an element of
Y(a_{0},a_{n})
. A monoid in
\mathbf{SetMat}_{E}
is just a category on
E
as described in the Introduction.

Any monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
gives a multicategory
\mathbf{Mult}({\mathcal{C}})
with the same objects: a morphism
f:\vec{a}\to b
is a
\mathcal{C}
-morphism
\bigotimes\vec{a}\rightarrow b
. Hermida
[
11
, §9.2]
showed that the multicategories so obtained are the
representable
ones.

Definition 7
.

In a multicategory, a
tensor
of a list of objects
\vec{b}
consists of an object
v
and morphism
p:\vec{b}\to v
such that, for any lists of objects
\vec{a}
and
\vec{c}
, object
x
and morphism
f:\vec{a},\vec{b},\vec{c}\to x
, there exists a unique morphism
g:\vec{a},v,\vec{c}\to x
with:

\begin{tabular}[c]{@{}c@{}}\begin{tabular}[b]{>{\centering} b{\@fullwidth} b{0pt}}\parbox[b]{31.83pt}{\centering$\vec{a},~\vec{b},~\vec{c}$\@add@centering}\@add@centering&\\[3.0pt]
\makebox[0.0pt]{}\makebox[0.0pt]{\hbox to32.23pt{\vbox to51.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 16.11499pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}

{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.98613pt}{31.16pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\makebox[0.0pt]{}\makebox[0.0pt]{\hbox to13.6pt{\vbox to24.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.79901pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}

Note that the tensor
(v,p)
is unique up to unique isomorphism. A multicategory is
representable
when every list of objects has a specified tensor.

Considering the above examples:

•

The multicategories
\mathbf{Set}
,
\mathbf{Ab}
and
\mathbf{SupLat}
are representable, with tensors given by cartesian products in
\mathbf{Set}
and respective tensor products in
\mathbf{Ab}
and
\mathbf{SupLat}
.

•

For a class
E
, the multicategory
\mathbf{SetMat}_{E}
is representable iff
E
is a set.

Lastly, we note that
any
category
\mathcal{C}
is a category of monoids. To this end, we write
\mathbf{Seq}({\mathcal{C}})
for the multicategory
5
5

5

Called a
sequential multicategory
in
[
12
]
, since a morphism is a sequence.
with the same objects as
\mathcal{C}
, where a morphism from
\vec{a}=(a_{0},\ldots,a_{n-1})
to object
x
is a list of
\mathcal{C}
-morphisms
(f_{i}:a_{i}\rightarrow x)_{i<n}
. Composition and identities are inherited from
\mathcal{C}
in the obvious way, and the category of monoids in
\mathbf{Seq}({\mathcal{C}})
is clearly isomorphic to
\mathcal{C}
.

IV
Intermediate level: skew multicategory

IV-A

Digression: relative monads

We leave the multicategory story for a while, to learn about the notion of relative monad. (Later, we shall explain how to see this as another monoid example.) We present it in a way that unifies the two versions from the literature.

Recall first that a monad on a category
\mathcal{C}
may (especially in computer science) be given as a
Kleisli triple

(T,\eta,{}^{*})
, where
T
is given on objects only,
\eta
is a family of maps
\eta_{x}:x\to Tx
and
∗
sends any morphism
f:x\rightarrow Ty
to its
Kleisli extension

f^{*}:Tx\rightarrow Ty
. The following definition is arranged in a similar way.

Definition 8
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be a bimodule from a category
\mathcal{C}
to a category
\mathcal{D}
. An
\mathcal{O}
-relative monad

(T,\eta,{}^{*})
consists of the following data:

•

For each
x\!\in\!\mathcal{C}
, a
\mathcal{D}
-object
Tx
and
\mathcal{O}
-morphism
\eta_{x}:x\rightarrow Tx
.

•

For each
x,y\!\in\!\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{O}
-morphism
k:x\rightarrow Ty
, a
\mathcal{D}
-morphism
{k}^{*}:Tx\rightarrow Ty
.

The following conditions must be satisfied.

•

For each
x,y\!\in\!\mathcal{C}
and
k:x\rightarrow Ty
, this triangle commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tx}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ty}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{*}}

•

For each
x\!\in\!\mathcal{C}
, the parallel pair

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tx}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tx}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{x}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}}
commutes, which is to say
\eta_{x}^{*}=\mathsf{id}_{x}
.

•

For
x,y,z\!\in\!\mathcal{C}
and
k:x\rightarrow Ty
and
l:y\rightarrow Tz
, this triangle commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tx}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ty}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tz}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(k;l^{*})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l^{*}}

Note that
\mathcal{C}
-morphisms are ignored in this definition—we could even take
\mathcal{O}
to be a bimodule from a
class
to a category. Although ignoring
\mathcal{C}
-morphisms may seem odd, the following result justifies it.

Proposition 1
.

For any
\mathcal{O}
-relative monad
(T,\eta,^{*})
, the function
T
uniquely extends to a functor
\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
making
\eta
and
∗
natural in the sense that the following conditions hold.

1.

For any
\mathcal{C}
-morphism
f:x\rightarrow y
, this square commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tx}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ty}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{y}}

2.

For any
f:u\rightarrow x
and
g:x\rightarrow Ty
, this triangle commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tu}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tx}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ty}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tf}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f;g)^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{*}}

3.

For any
g:x\rightarrow Ty
and
h:y\rightarrow z
, this triangle commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tx}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ty}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tz}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g;Th)^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Th}

See the Appendix for the proof.

Now we recover the two versions from the literature as special cases via the constructions of bimodules from functors in Definition
2
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
be categories and consider a functor
J:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
.
\mathsf{Forward}_{J}
-relative monads are exactly the
“relative monads”
introduced by Altenkirch, Chapman and Uustalu in
[
3
]
. For example, let
J
be the embedding of the small category of (a skeleton of) finite sets and functions into
\mathbf{Set}
. Then a
\mathsf{Forward}_{J}
-relative monad, also called a
“cartesian operad”
or
“abstract clone”
, corresponds to a finitary monad
T
on
\mathbf{Set}
. We think of
T([n])
as the set of all
n
-ary operations. This example generalizes to the notion of
“monad with arities”

[
13
]
.

For a functor
U:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
,
\mathsf{Backward}_{U}
-relative monads are exactly the kind introduced by Spivey in
[
4
]
. To illustrate this case, let
U
be the forgetful functor from
\mathbf{Grp}
(the category of all groups) to
\mathbf{Set}
. Since
U
is faithful, we see that a
\mathsf{Backward}_{U}
-relative monad is a Kleisli triple (or monad) on
\mathbf{Set}
equipped with a group structure on
TA
for each set
A
, such that the Kleisli extension of each function
A\rightarrow TB
is a homomorphism
TA\rightarrow TB
.
Since this functor
U
has a left adjoint
F
(viz. the free group functor), the notion of
\mathsf{Backward}_{U}
-relative monad coincides with that of
\mathsf{Forward}_{F}
-relative monad.

Let us see an example where
U
has no left adjoint. Say that a
dynamical complete lattice
is a complete lattice equipped with an endofunction called the
dynamics
. Write
\mathbf{DynSup}
for the category of all dynamic complete lattices and
sup-homomorphisms
, i.e. functions that preserve all suprema and the dynamics.

Proposition 2
.

\mathbf{DynSup}
has no initial object.

See the Appendix for the proof. By Proposition
2
, the forgetful functor
U:\mathbf{DynSup}\rightarrow\mathbf{Set}
has no left adjoint, as left adjoints preserve initiality. Nonetheless, the story is the same as before: a
\mathsf{Backward}_{U}
-relative monad is a Kleisli triple (or monad) on
\mathbf{Set}
equipped with a dynamical suplattice structure on
TA
for each set
A
, such that the Kleisli extension of each function
A\rightarrow TB
is a sup-homomorphism
TA\rightarrow TB
. For example, let
T
send each set
A
to the free (with respect to sup-homomorphisms) dynamic complete lattice on
A
whose dynamics preserves all suprema. Explicitly, this is
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}\times A)
ordered by inclusion, with dynamics sending
c\!\in\!\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}\times A)
to
\{{(n+1,x)\mid(n,x)\!\in\!c}\}
and unit sending
x\!\in\!A
to
\{{(0,x)}\}
.

The following
“naturality”
formulation appeared in
[
4
]
.

Proposition 3
.

For a functor
U:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
, a
\mathsf{Backward}_{U}
-relative monad can be represented as a functor
T:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
together with natural transformations
\eta:\mathsf{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\rightarrow UT
and
\mu:TUT\rightarrow T
such that the following diagrams commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TUT}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UT}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UTUT}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TUTUT}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TUT}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UT}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TUT}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta UT}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{id}_{UT}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu UT}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TU\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}

IV-B

Monoids in a skew multicategory

It is now time to generalize the notion of multicategory. We write
\vec{a},\vec{b},\ldots
for possibly empty object lists, and
\accentset{\twoheadrightarrow}{a},\accentset{\twoheadrightarrow}{b},\ldots
for nonempty object lists.

Definition 9
.

A
left-skew multicategory

\mathcal{C}
consists of the following data.

•

A class
|{\mathcal{C}}|
of objects.

•

For any object list
\vec{a}
and object
x
, a set of
loose
morphisms
\mathcal{C}(\vec{a};x)
. Such a morphism is written
f:\vec{a}\rightarrow x
or pictorially as

~\vec{a}~

f

x

•

For any nonempty object list
\accentset{\twoheadrightarrow}{a}
and object
x
, a set of
tight
morphisms
\mathcal{C}(\smash{\lfloor}{\accentset{\twoheadrightarrow}{a}};x)
. Such a morphism is written
f:\smash{\lfloor}{\accentset{\twoheadrightarrow}{a}}\rightarrow x
or pictorially as

\smash{\lfloor}{\accentset{\twoheadrightarrow}{a}}~

f

x

•

Loosening:
An operation sending each tight morphism
f:\smash{\lfloor}{\accentset{\twoheadrightarrow}{a}}\rightarrow x
to a loose morphism
\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\acute{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}:\accentset{\twoheadrightarrow}{a}\rightarrow x
:

The following conditions must be satisfied.

•

Loosening commutes with tight composition and with mixed composition.

•

Left identity for each kind of composition:
A
,
B
,
C
.

•

Right identity for each kind of morphism: loose and tight.

•

Associativity laws:

\frac{\mathrm{A}}{\mathrm{A}},\frac{\mathrm{B}}{\mathrm{B}},\frac{\mathrm{A}}{\mathrm{C}},\frac{\mathrm{A}}{\mathrm{B}}

•

Interchange laws:

\text{\small{\rm{A}{A}}},\text{\small{\rm{B}{C}}},\text{\small{\rm{C}{C}}}

where letters
A
,
B
,
C
indicate the composition forms; associativity and interchange laws impose that either order in which the composition may be performed produce the same result.

Any left skew-monoidal category
(\mathcal{C},\otimes,I,\alpha,\lambda,\rho)
gives a left skew multicategory with the same objects: a loose morphism
f:\vec{a}\to b
is a
\mathcal{C}
-morphism
\bigotimes\vec{a}\rightarrow b
, and a tight morphism
f:x,\vec{a}\to b
is a
\mathcal{C}
-morphism
\bigotimes_{x}\vec{a}\rightarrow b
. Bourke and Lack showed that the left skew multicategories so obtained are the
left-representable
ones
[
5
, Theorem 6.1]
.

Definition 10
.

of a non-empty list of objects

, object
x
and morphism

g:\smash{\lfloor}{v},\vec{b}\to x

Note that this is weaker than the condition imposed in Definition
7
, but that these left tensors are nonetheless unique up to unique tight isomorphism. A left-skew multicategory is
left-representable
when all non-empty lists of objects have both kinds of left tensor and the empty list has a loose tensor.

Remark 1
.

Offord provides string diagram syntax for left-skew multicategories in
[
14
]
which is topologically equivalent to our trapezate syntax. Offord also includes syntactic operations for tensors in order to specialize to the representable case.

Now let us form a 2-category of left-skew multicategories. Given left-skew multicategories
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
, we proceed as follows. A
map

F:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
sends objects to objects, loose morphisms to loose morphisms, and tight morphisms to tight morphisms in the obvious way. Given maps
F,G:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
, a
2-cell

\alpha:F\Rightarrow G
sends each
\mathcal{C}
-object
x
to a tight map
\alpha_{x}:\smash{\lfloor}{Fx}\to Gx
, satisfying the evident naturality condition for every (loose or tight)
\mathcal{C}
-morphism.

We may now generalize Definition
6
to the skew setting.

Definition 11
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a left-skew multicategory.

1.

A
monoid
in
\mathcal{C}
consists of a
\mathcal{C}
-object
x
, a tight map
m:\smash{\lfloor}{x,x}\to x
(the
multiplication
), and a nullary map
e:\to x
(the
unit
), satisfying the following equations.

\end{tabular}\end{tabular}

3.

We write
\mathbf{Mon}({\mathcal{C}})
for the category of all monoids in
\mathcal{C}
and homomorphisms.

In the case where
\mathcal{C}
arises from a left-skew monoidal category (i.e., it is left-representable), we can unpack Definition
11
and obtain the notion called
“skew-monoid”
in
[
3
, §3]
. In the absence of any competing notion of monoid in this setting, we propose that these deserve to be known simply as monoids.

IV-C

Examples of monoids in left-skew multicategories

First we explain how multicategories are a special case of left-skew multicategories.

Definition 12
.

Any multicategory
\mathcal{C}
gives a left-skew multicategory
\iota_{\mathsf{Left}}(\mathcal{C})
with the same objects. Both the loose and the tight hom-sets are just
\mathcal{C}
-hom-sets, and loosening is identity. Composition and identities are inherited from
\mathcal{C}
.
We extend
\iota_{\mathsf{Left}}
to a 2-functor in the obvious way.

Proposition 4
.

For a multicategory
\mathcal{C}
, the category of monoids in
\mathcal{C}
is precisely that of monoids in
\iota_{\mathsf{Left}}(\mathcal{C})
.

Now we turn to our main example of a monoid in a left-skew multicategory:

Theorem 1
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be a bimodule from a category
\mathcal{C}
to a category
\mathcal{D}
. Then an
\mathcal{O}
-relative monad is precisely a monoid in the following hyperlarge skew-multicategory.

•

An object is a functor
\mathcal{C}\rightarrow{\mathcal{D}}
.

•

A morphism
f:T_{0},\ldots,T_{n-1}\rightarrow H
is an extranatural family of maps

\mathcal{O}(x_{0},T_{0}x_{1})\times\cdots\times\mathcal{O}(x_{n-1},T_{n-1}x_{n})\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(x_{0},Hx_{n})

•

A morphism
f:\smash{\lfloor}{S,T_{0},\ldots,T_{n-1}}\rightarrow H
is an extranatural family of maps

\mathcal{O}(x_{0},T_{0}x_{1})\times\cdots\times\mathcal{O}(x_{n-1},T_{n-1}x_{n})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(Sx_{0},Hx_{n})

•

Loosening, identities and composition are defined in the evident way.

Proof.

Immediate from Proposition
1
.
∎

Consider the above left-skew multicategory in the case that
\mathcal{O}
has the form
\mathsf{Backward}_{U}
for a functor
U:\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
. The Yoneda lemma allows us to represent:

•

a morphism
f:T_{0},\ldots,T_{n-1}\rightarrow H
as a natural transformation
UT_{0}\cdots UT_{n-1}\rightarrow UH

•

a morphism
f:\smash{\lfloor}{S,T_{0},\ldots,T_{n-1}}\rightarrow H
as a natural transformation
SUT_{0}\cdots UT_{n-1}\rightarrow H

with loosening, identities and composition given in the evident way.
This immediately yields Proposition
3
.

Note that Arkor and McDermott
[
15
]
have given a different notion of skew-multicategory, in which
\mathcal{O}
-relative monads can be seen as monoids in the case that
\mathcal{O}
is of the form
\mathsf{Forward}_{F}
.

V
Advanced level: bi-skew multicategory

V-A

Digression: call-by-push-value sequencing

We leave the skew-multicategory story for a while, to formulate a categorical semantics for Figure
2
. (Later, we shall explain how
to see this as another monoid example.)

Firstly, a
cartesian base
for CBPV modelling consists of a cartesian category
\mathcal{C}
, classes
X
and
Y
, and functions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{|{\mathcal{C}}|}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

Our intended interpretation is as follows.

•

A value type
A
will denote an element
[\![{A}]\!]\!\in\!X
.

•

A computation type
\underline{B}
will denote an element
\underline{B}\!\in\!Y
, using

\displaystyle[\![{FA}]\!]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle F[\![{A}]\!]

•

A typing context
\Gamma
will denote a
\mathcal{C}
-object
[\![{\Gamma}]\!]
, using

\displaystyle[\![{\mathsf{nil}}]\!]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle[\![{\Gamma,x:A}]\!]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\![{\Gamma}]\!]\times j[\![{A}]\!]

•

A value
\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{v}}V:A
denotes a
\mathcal{C}
-morphism
[\![{\Gamma}]\!]\rightarrow j[\![{A}]\!]
.

•

A substitution
k:\Gamma\rightarrow\Delta
, assigning to each declaration
(x:A)\!\in\!\Gamma
a value
\Delta\vdash^{\mathsf{v}}k_{x}:A
, will denote a
\mathcal{C}
-morphism
[\![{k}]\!]:[\![{\Delta}]\!]\rightarrow[\![{\Gamma}]\!]
.

To interpret computations, we give the following structure, which closely follows Figure
2
.

Definition 13
.

On a cartesian base
(\mathcal{C},X,Y,j,F)
, a
CBPV sequencing model
consists of the following data.

•

A bimodule
\mathcal{O}
from
\mathcal{C}
to
Y
.

•

For each
A\!\in\!X
, a function

\mathsf{ret}^{\Gamma}_{A}:\mathcal{C}(\Gamma,jA)\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(\Gamma,FA)

natural in
\Gamma\!\in\!\mathcal{C}
.

•

For each
A\!\in\!X
and
\underline{B}\!\in\!Y
, a function

\mathsf{to}^{\Gamma}_{{A},{\underline{B}}}:\mathcal{O}(\Gamma,FA)\times\mathcal{O}(\Gamma\times jA,\underline{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(\Gamma,\underline{B})

natural in
\Gamma\!\in\!\mathcal{C}
.

The following laws must be satisfied.

•

For any
\mathcal{C}
-morphism
V:\Gamma\rightarrow j(A)
and
\mathcal{O}
-morphism
M:\Gamma\times jA\rightarrow\underline{B}
,
we have

\displaystyle(\mathsf{ret}\ V)\ \mathsf{to}\ M

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle{\mathsf{id}_{\Gamma},V}\rangle\,;\,M

•

For any
\mathcal{O}
-morphism
M:\Gamma\rightarrow FA
, we have

\displaystyle M\ \mathsf{to}\ (\mathsf{ret}\ \pi^{\prime}_{{\Gamma},{A}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle M

•

For any
\mathcal{O}
-morphisms
M:\Gamma\rightarrow FA
and
N:\Gamma\times jA\rightarrow FB
and
P:\Gamma\times jB\rightarrow\underline{C}
, we have

\displaystyle(M\ \mathsf{to}\ N)\ \mathsf{to}\ P

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle M\ \mathsf{to}\ (N\ \mathsf{to}\ (\pi_{{\Gamma},{jA}}\times jB;P))

The intention is that a computation
\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{c}}M:\underline{B}
denotes an
\mathcal{O}
-morphism
[\![{M}]\!]:[\![{\Gamma}]\!]\rightarrow[\![{\underline{B}}]\!]
, using

\displaystyle[\![{\mathtt{return}\ V}]\!]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsf{ret}\ [\![{V}]\!]

\displaystyle[\![{M\ \mathtt{to}\ x.\ N}]\!]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\![{M}]\!]\ \mathsf{to}\ [\![{N}]\!]

for
\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{v}}V:A
and
\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{c}}M:FA
and
\Gamma,x:A\vdash^{\mathsf{c}}N:\underline{B}
. Intuitively, the naturality requirements on
\mathsf{ret}
and
\mathsf{to}
correspond to the fact that the term constructors
\mathtt{return}
and
\mathtt{to}
commute with substitution. Note that, in the RHS of the associative law in Figure
2
, the term
P
is implicitly weakened by
x
, and this is reflected in the RHS of the associative law in Definition
13
.

We claim that any CBPV sequencing model gives a semantics that validates the laws in Figure
2
. To make this precise, say that a
basic CBPV signature
consists of:

•

a set of value types

•

a set of computation types

•

a set of value constructors
f:(A_{i})_{i<n}\rightarrow B

•

a set of computation constructors
g:(A_{i})_{i<n}\rightarrow\underline{B}
.

Such a signature generates terms using the typing rules in Figure
2
and additionally

\begin{array}[]{c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 1.61488pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty(\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{v}}V_{i}:A_{i})_{i<n}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=72.16646pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$f:(A_{i})_{i<n}\rightarrow B$}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{v}}f(V_{i})_{i<n}:B$}}}}\\
\\
{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty(\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{v}}V_{i}:A_{i})_{i<n}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=68.93669pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt$g:(A_{i})_{i<n}\rightarrow\underline{B}$}}}\hbox{\kern 1.79759pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash^{\mathsf{c}}g(V_{i})_{i<n}:\underline{B}$}}}}\end{array}

Given a CBPV sequencing model
(\mathcal{O},\mathsf{ret},\mathsf{to})
on a cartesian base
(\mathcal{C},X,Y,j,F)
, an
interpretation
of the signature interprets value types in
X
, computation types in
Y
, value constructors as
\mathcal{C}
-morphisms, and computation constructors as
\mathcal{O}
-morphisms. We then obtain the denotation of each term, and our claim is that the three laws in Figure
2
are validated. It is proved in the usual way, using a substitution lemma.

V-B

The framework

To bring together all our examples, we give a new definition generalizing both left-skew and right-skew multicategory.

Definition 14
.

A
bi-skew multicategory

\mathcal{C}
consists of the following data:

•

A class
|{\mathcal{C}}|
of objects.

•

For any object list
\vec{a}
and object
x
, a set of
loose-loose
morphisms
\mathcal{C}(\vec{a};x)
. Such a morphism is written
f:\vec{a}\rightarrow x
or pictorially as,

~\vec{a}~

f

x

•

For any nonempty object list
\accentset{\twoheadrightarrow}{a}
and object
x
, a set of
tight-loose
morphisms
\mathcal{C}(\smash{\lfloor}{\accentset{\twoheadrightarrow}{a}};x)
. Such a morphism is written
f:\smash{\lfloor}{\accentset{\twoheadrightarrow}{a}}\rightarrow x
or pictorially as,

\smash{\lfloor}{\accentset{\twoheadrightarrow}{a}}~

f

x

•

For any nonempty object list
\accentset{\twoheadrightarrow}{a}
and object
x
, a set of
loose-tight
morphisms
\mathcal{C}({\accentset{\twoheadrightarrow}{a}}\rfloor;x)
. Such a morphism is written
f:{\accentset{\twoheadrightarrow}{a}}\rfloor\rightarrow x
or pictorially as,

~{\accentset{\twoheadrightarrow}{a}}\rfloor

f

x

•

For any nonempty object list
\accentset{\twoheadrightarrow}{a}
and object
x
, a set of
tight-tight
morphisms
\mathcal{C}(\lfloor{\accentset{\twoheadrightarrow}{a}}\rfloor;x)
. Such a morphism is written
f:\lfloor{\accentset{\twoheadrightarrow}{a}}\rfloor\rightarrow x
or pictorially as,

\lfloor{\accentset{\twoheadrightarrow}{a}}\rfloor

f

x

•

An operation sending

•

Tight-tight identities:
For each
\mathcal{C}
-object
x
,
\mathsf{id}:\lfloor{x}\rfloor\to x
.

•

Composition:
Considering the forms in Table
I
, the following conditions must be satisfied.

–

Left-loosening and right-loosening commute, i.e. for any
f:\lfloor{\accentset{\twoheadrightarrow}{a}}\rfloor\to x
, the loose-loose morphisms
\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\grave{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\acute{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and
\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\acute{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\grave{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
are equal—we write either as
\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\hat{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
.

–

Left-loosening commutes with
B, D, E, F, I
.

–

Right-loosening commutes with
C, D, F, H, I
.

–

Left identity for each kind of composition.

–

Right identity for each kind of morphism.

–

Associativity laws:

\frac{\mathrm{E}}{\mathrm{E}},\frac{\mathrm{G}}{\mathrm{G}},\frac{\mathrm{E}}{\mathrm{F}},\frac{\mathrm{G}}{\mathrm{H}},\frac{\mathrm{F}}{\mathrm{I}},\frac{\mathrm{H}}{\mathrm{I}},\frac{\mathrm{I}}{\mathrm{I}}

–

Interchange laws:

\text{\small{\rm{A}{A}}},\text{\small{\rm{E}{B}}},\text{\small{\rm{B}{B}}},\text{\small{\rm{C}{G}}},\text{\small{\rm{C}{C}}},\text{\small{\rm{F}{H}}},\text{\small{\rm{F}{D}}},\text{\small{\rm{D}{H}}},\text{\small{\rm{D}{D}}}

I

\lfloor{\accentset{\twoheadrightarrow}{a}}\rfloor

f

\lfloor{x}\rfloor

g

y

TABLE I
:
Composition forms in a bi-skew multicategory. The labels are used in the associativity and interchange laws in Definition
14

Now let us form a 2-category of bi-skew multicategories. Given bi-skew multicategories
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
, we proceed as follows. A
map

F:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
sends objects to objects, and (for each kind) morphisms to morphisms, in the obvious way. Given maps
F,G:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
, a
2-cell

\alpha:F\Rightarrow G
sends each
\mathcal{C}
-object
x
to a tight-tight map
\alpha_{x}:\lfloor{Fx}\rfloor\to Gx
, satisfying the naturality condition for
\mathcal{C}
-morphisms of each kind.

Remark 2
.

Readers familiar with the work of Bourke and Lack
[
5
]
may notice that the variants of multicategories we present here can be viewed as instances of their
\mathcal{T}
-multicategory framework, where
\mathcal{T}
is a
\mathbf{Cat}
-enriched operad.

Ordinary multicategories are
\mathbbm{1}
-multicategories, where
\mathbbm{1}
is the terminal
\mathbf{Cat}
-operad. Bourke and Lack define an operad
\mathcal{R}
such that left-skew multicategories are
\mathcal{R}
-multicategories. Likewise, right-skew multicategories are
\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{rev}}
-multicategories, where
\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{rev}}
is the ‘reverse’ of
\mathcal{R}
obtained by reversing the indexing on the composition rule (without changing
\mathcal{R}_{n}
). Finally, bi-skew multicategories are
\mathcal{R}\times\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{rev}}
-multicategories.

Now we come to the main definition of the paper.

Definition 15
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a bi-skew multicategory.

1.

A
monoid
in
\mathcal{C}
consists of a
\mathcal{C}
-object
x
, a map
m:\lfloor{x,x}\rfloor\to x
(the
multiplication
), and a nullary map
e:\to x
(the
unit
), satisfying the following equations:

3.

We write
\mathbf{Mon}({\mathcal{C}})
for the category of all monoids in
\mathcal{C}
and homomorphisms.

As in Definition
12
and Proposition
4
, we must see that this generalizes our previous notions:

Definition 16
.

Any left-skew multicategory
\mathcal{C}
gives a bi-skew multicategory
\iota_{\mathsf{LtoBi}}(\mathcal{C})
with the same objects. Both the loose-loose and loose-tight hom-sets are just loose
\mathcal{C}
-hom-sets, and right-loosening is identity. Likewise, the tight-loose and tight-tight hom-sets are just tight
\mathcal{C}
-hom-sets, and left-loosening is loosening. Composition and identities are inherited from
\mathcal{C}
. We extend
\iota_{\mathsf{LtoBi}}
to a 2-functor in the obvious way.

Proposition 5
.

For a left-skew multicategory
\mathcal{C}
, the category of monoids in
\mathcal{C}
is precisely that of monoids in
\iota_{\mathsf{LtoBi}}(\mathcal{C})
.

V-C

Example: categories on a class span

Theorem 2
.

A category on a class span
(E,A,B,f,g)
is precisely a monoid in the following bi-skew multicategory.

•

An object is a set-valued matrix
X:A\,\to\hskip-6.49994pt\shortmid\hskip 5.0ptB
.

•

A morphism
X_{0},\ldots,X_{n-1}\rightarrow Y
consists of a function

\begin{array}[]{c}X_{0}(fi_{0},gi_{1})\,\times\,\cdots\,\times\,X_{n-1}(fi_{n-1},gi_{n})\\
\quad\,\rightarrow\,Y(fi_{0},gi_{n})\end{array}

for all
i_{0},\ldots,i_{n}\!\in\!E
.

•

A morphism
\smash{\lfloor}{W,X_{0},\ldots,X_{n-1}}\rightarrow Y
consists of a function

\begin{array}[]{c}W(a,gi_{0})\,\times\\
X_{0}(fi_{0},gi_{1})\,\times\,\cdots\,\times\,X_{n-1}(fi_{n-1},gi_{n})\\
\quad\,\rightarrow\,Y(a,gi_{n})\end{array}

for all
a\!\in\!A
and
i_{0},\ldots,i_{n}\!\in\!E
.

•

A morphism
{X_{0},\ldots,X_{n-1},V}\rfloor\rightarrow Y
consists of a function

\begin{array}[]{c}X_{0}(fi_{0},gi_{1})\,\times\,\cdots\times X_{n-1}(fi_{n-1},gi_{n})\\
\,\times\,V(fi_{n},b)\\
\quad\,\rightarrow\,Y(fi_{0},b)\end{array}

for all
i_{0},\ldots,i_{n}\!\in\!E
and
b\!\in\!B
.

•

A morphism
\lfloor{W,X_{0},\ldots,X_{n-1},V}\rfloor\rightarrow Y
consists of a function

\begin{array}[]{c}W(a,gi_{0})\,\times\\
\quad X_{0}(fi_{0},gi_{1})\,\times\,\cdots\,\times\,X_{n-1}(fi_{n-1},gi_{n})\\
\,\times\,V(fi_{n},b)\\
\quad\,\rightarrow\,Y(a,b)\end{array}

for all
a\!\in\!A
and
i_{0},\ldots,i_{n}\!\in\!E
and
b\!\in\!B
.

•

A morphism
\lfloor{W}\rfloor\rightarrow Y
consists of a function

W(a,b)\,\rightarrow\,Y(a,b)

for all
a\!\in\!A
and
b\!\in\!B
.

•

Left-loosening, right-loosening, composition and identities are defined in the evident way.

V-D

Example: modelling of CBPV sequencing

To analyze CBPV sequencing models, we consider representable presheaves. For a category
\mathcal{C}
, the hyperlarge category
[\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{op}},\mathbf{Set}]
is called the category of
left
\mathcal{C}
-modules
, or
presheaves
. Any
\mathcal{C}
-object
a
gives a presheaf
\mathcal{C}(-,a)
, which is called
representable
. In the case that
\mathcal{C}
is cartesian, we can always exponentiate by a representable presheaf:

Proposition 6
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a cartesian category. For any left
\mathcal{C}
-module
H
and
\mathcal{C}
-object
a
, the exponential of
H
by
\mathcal{C}(-,a)
is
H(-\times a)
. This means that we have a bijective correspondence, natural in the left module
G
, between

•

natural transformations from
\mathcal{C}(-,a)\times G
to
H
, which we call
“long-form”

•

natural transformations from
G
to
H(-\times a)
, which we call
“short-form”
.

Explicitly: a long-form
\alpha
corresponds to the short-form that, at
\mathcal{C}
-object
x
, sends
r:x\rightarrow
to
\alpha_{x\times a}(\pi^{\prime}_{x,a},\pi_{x,a};r)
. Conversely, a short-form
\beta
corresponds to the long-form that, at
\mathcal{C}
-object
y
, sends
f:y\rightarrow a
and
s:y\rightarrow
to
\langle{\mathsf{id}_{y},f}\rangle;(\beta_{y}(s))
.

Proof.

By direct calculation.
∎

Theorem 3
.

On a cartesian base
(\mathcal{C},X,Y,j,F)
, a CBPV sequencing model is precisely a monoid in the following bi-skew multicategory.

•

An object is a bimodule from
\mathcal{C}
to
Y
.

•

A morphism
\mathcal{O}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{O}_{n-1}\rightarrow\Omega
is a choice of functions,

\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{O}_{0}(\Gamma\times jA_{0},FA_{1})\times\cdots\times\mathcal{O}_{n-1}(\Gamma\times jA_{n-1},FA_{n})\\
\quad\,\rightarrow\,\Omega(\Gamma\times jA_{0},FA_{n})\end{array}

natural in
\Gamma\in\mathcal{C}
, for all
A_{0},\ldots,A_{n}\!\in\!X
.

•

A morphism
\smash{\lfloor}{\Phi,\mathcal{O}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{O}_{n-1}}\rightarrow\Omega
is a choice of functions,

\begin{array}[]{c}\Phi(\Gamma,FA_{0})\,\times\\
\mathcal{O}_{0}(\Gamma\times jA_{0},FA_{1})\times\cdots\times\mathcal{O}_{n-1}(\Gamma\times jA_{n-1},FA_{n})\\
\quad\,\rightarrow\,\Omega(\Gamma,FA_{n})\end{array}

natural in
\Gamma\in\mathcal{C}
, for all
A_{0},\ldots,A_{n}\!\in\!X
.

•

A morphism
{\mathcal{O}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{O}_{n-1},\Psi}\rfloor\rightarrow\Omega
is a choice of functions,

\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{O}_{0}(\Gamma\times jA_{0},FA_{1})\times\cdots\times\mathcal{O}_{n-1}(\Gamma\times jA_{n-1},FA_{n})\\
\quad\times\,\Psi(\Gamma\times jA_{n},\underline{B})\\
\quad\,\rightarrow\,\ \Omega(\Gamma\times jA_{0},\underline{B})\end{array}

natural in
\Gamma\in\mathcal{C}
, for all
A_{0},\ldots,A_{n}\!\in\!X
and
\underline{B}\!\in\!Y
.

•

A morphism
\lfloor{\Phi,\mathcal{O}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{O}_{n-1},\Psi}\rfloor\rightarrow\Omega
is a choice of functions,

\begin{array}[]{c}\Phi(\Gamma,FA_{0})\,\times\\
\mathcal{O}_{0}(\Gamma\times jA_{0},FA_{1})\times\cdots\times\mathcal{O}_{n-1}(\Gamma\times jA_{n-1},FA_{n})\\
\quad\times\,\Psi(\Gamma\times jA_{n},\underline{B})\\
\quad\,\rightarrow\,\ \Omega(\Gamma,\underline{B})\end{array}

natural in
\Gamma\in\mathcal{C}
, for all
A_{0},\ldots,A_{n}\!\in\!X
and
\underline{B}\!\in\!Y
.

•

A morphism
\lfloor{\Phi}\rfloor\rightarrow\Omega
is a choice of functions,

\Phi(\Gamma,\underline{B})\ \,\rightarrow\,\ \Omega(\Gamma,\underline{B})

natural in
\Gamma\in\mathcal{C}
, for all
\underline{B}\!\in\!Y
.

•

Left-loosening, right-loosening, composition and identities are defined in the evident way.

Proof.

First note that a morphism
\mathcal{O}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{O}_{n-1}\rightarrow\Omega
can be expressed in long-form, as a function

\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma\times jA_{0})\times\\
\mathcal{O}_{0}(\Gamma\times jA_{0},FA_{1})\times\cdots\times\mathcal{O}_{n-1}(\Gamma\times jA_{n-1},FA_{n})\\
\quad\,\rightarrow\,\Omega(\Gamma,FA_{n})\end{array}

natural in
\Gamma\in\mathcal{C}
, for all
A_{0},\ldots,A_{n}\!\in\!X
. Likewise a morphism
{\mathcal{O}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{O}_{n-1},\Psi}\rfloor\rightarrow\Omega
can be expressed in long-form, as a function

\begin{array}[]{c}\mathcal{C}(\Gamma,jA_{0})\times\\
\mathcal{O}_{0}(\Gamma\times jA_{0},FA_{1})\times\cdots\times\mathcal{O}_{n-1}(\Gamma\times jA_{n-1},FA_{n})\\
\quad\times\,\Psi(\Gamma\times jA_{n},\underline{B})\\
\quad\,\rightarrow\,\ \Omega(\Gamma,\underline{B})\end{array}

natural in
\Gamma\in\mathcal{C}
, for all
A_{0},\ldots,A_{n}\!\in\!X
and
\underline{B}\!\in\!Y
. We accordingly use the construction of Proposition
6
to convert Definition
13
as follows. A
short-form CBPV sequencing model
consists of the following data.

•

A bimodule
\mathcal{O}
from
\mathcal{C}
to
Y
.

•

For each
A\!\in\!X
, a function

\mathsf{retpi}:1\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(\Gamma\times A,FA)
natural in
\Gamma
.

•

For each
A\!\in\!X
and
\underline{B}\!\in\!Y
, a function

\mathsf{to}^{\Gamma}_{{A},{\underline{B}}}:\mathcal{O}(\Gamma,FA)\times\mathcal{O}(\Gamma\times jA,\underline{B})\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(\Gamma,\underline{B})

natural in
\Gamma\!\in\!\mathcal{C}
.

The following laws must be satisfied.

•

For any
\mathcal{O}
-morphism
M:\Gamma\times jA\rightarrow\underline{B}
,
we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{retpi}\ \mathsf{to}\ (\pi_{\Gamma,jA}\times jA);M

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle M

•

For any
\mathcal{O}
-morphism
M:\Gamma\rightarrow FA
, we have

\displaystyle M\ \mathsf{to}\ \mathsf{retpi}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle M

•

Associativity as in Definition
13
.

This is easily seen to be a monoid in the stated bi-skew multicategory.
∎

VI
Unbiased monoids

We now give an alternative definition of monoid, which explicitly includes
n
-ary composition for every
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, not just for
n=0
and
n=2
. The term
“unbiased”
is taken from
[
16
]
.

Definition 17
.

1.

For a multicategory
\mathcal{C}
, the
category of unbiased monoids
, written
\mathbf{UnbiasMon}({\mathcal{C}})
, is the hom-category (in the 2-category of multicategories) from the terminal multicategory
1
to
\mathcal{C}
.

2.

Likewise if
\mathcal{C}
is a left-skew or right-skew or bi-skew multicategory.

Let us unpack this definition. For
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
and an object
x
, we write
x^{(n)}
for the list consisting of
x
repeated
n
times.

•

In a multicategory
\mathcal{C}
, an unbiased monoid consists of the following data.

–

A
\mathcal{C}
-object
x
.

–

For each
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, a
\mathcal{C}
-morphism
m_{n}:x^{(n)}\rightarrow x
, known as
n
-ary multiplication
.

These must be compatible with identities (i.e.
m_{1}=\mathsf{id}_{x}
) and composition. The
biased counterpart
is the monoid
(x,m_{2},m_{0})
. Lastly, a
homomorphism
from
(x,\ldots)
to
(y,\ldots)
is a
\mathcal{C}
-morphism
x\rightarrow y
compatible with each kind of multiplication.

•

In a left-skew multicategory
\mathcal{C}
, an unbiased monoid consists of the following data.

–

A
\mathcal{C}
-object
x
.

–

For each
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, a
\mathcal{C}
-morphism
m^{\mathsf{L}}_{n}:x^{(n)}\rightarrow x
.

–

For each
n>0
, a
\mathcal{C}
-morphism
m^{\mathsf{T}}_{n}:\smash{\lfloor}{x^{(n)}}\rightarrow x
.

These must be compatible with loosening, identities, and each kind of composition. The
biased counterpart
is the monoid
(x,m^{\mathsf{T}}_{2},m^{\mathsf{L}}_{0})
. Lastly, a
homomorphism
from
(x,\ldots)
to
(y,\ldots)
is a
\mathcal{C}
-morphism
\smash{\lfloor}{x}\rightarrow y
compatible with each kind of multiplication.

•

In a bi-skew multicategory
\mathcal{C}
, an unbiased monoid consists of the following data.

–

A
\mathcal{C}
-object
x
.

–

For each
n\!\in\!\mathbb{N}
, a
\mathcal{C}
-morphism
m^{\mathsf{LL}}_{n}:x^{(n)}\rightarrow x
.

–

For each
n>0
, a
\mathcal{C}
-morphism
m^{\mathsf{TL}}_{n}:\smash{\lfloor}{x^{(n)}}\rightarrow x
.

–

For each
n>0
, a
\mathcal{C}
-morphism
m^{\mathsf{LT}}_{n}:{x^{(n)}}\rfloor\rightarrow x
.

–

For each
n>0
, a
\mathcal{C}
-morphism
m^{\mathsf{TT}}_{n}:\lfloor{x^{(n)}}\rfloor\rightarrow x
.

These must be compatible with loosening, identities, and each kind of composition. The
biased counterpart
is the monoid
(x,m^{\mathsf{TT}}_{2},m^{\mathsf{LL}}_{0})
. Lastly, a
homomorphism
from
(x,\ldots)
to
(y,\ldots)
is a
\mathcal{C}
-morphism
\lfloor{x}\rfloor\rightarrow y
compatible with each kind of multiplication.

(In the last case, the reason the biased counterpart is a monoid is that both sides of the left identity law are
m^{\mathsf{LT}}_{1}
, both sides of the right identity
m^{\mathsf{TL}}_{1}
and both sides of the associativity law are
m^{\mathsf{TT}}_{3}
. Similarly for the other cases.)

Theorem 4

(Coherence for monoids)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a multicategory, left-skew multicategory, right-skew multicategory or bi-skew multicategory. Then we have an isomorphism
\mathbf{UnbiasMon}({\mathcal{C}})\cong\mathbf{Mon}({\mathcal{C}})
sending an unbiased monoid to its biased counterpart and a homomorphism to itself.

See the Appendix for the proof.

VII
Conclusion and further work

Progressing through three levels of generality, we have seen that many different notions turn out to be instances of monoids in the suitably expanded context of left-skew or bi-skew multicategories, including that of relative monad, category on a class span, and CBPV sequencing model. The key to achieving this was a formalism in which left and right factors are given a special status, realised as a distinction between tight and loose morphisms.

We do not claim that the resulting notion of monoid (Definition
15
) is the maximally general definition that happens to be backwards-compatible with established definitions of monoid, although we intend to examine that question in future. However, we stress that the essential structure appearing in each successive generalization took the form of (an object equipped with) something resembling a unit and something resembling a multiplication satisfying left and right identity and associativity laws, with just one instance of each law needed in each case (for example, we did not need multiple associativity axioms). Our analysis shows these to be exactly the requirements of a monoid in a bi-skew multicategory, and we know of no example that ‘looks like a monoid’ in this sense which does not fit the bi-skew framework. We accordingly challenge the community to find examples justifying further generality.

So far we have not asked the question, “What is a monoid action?” An answer would allow algebras of a relative monad to be treated in a common framework with the algebras for ordinary monads, amongst other examples. This would have applications in programming language theory, such as to the syntax with binding of Ahrens
[
17
]
. We expect the appropriate setting to be a suitable notion of multi
act
egory, comparable to the one developed by Arkor and McDermott
[
15
]
.

Proof of Proposition
2
.
Given an initial object
(C,\zeta)
, Hartogs’ lemma yields an ordinal
\alpha
such that there is no injection
\alpha\rightarrow C
. Form the dynamic complete lattice
R
given by the set of ordinals
\leqslant\alpha
, with dynamics sending
\beta<\alpha
to
\mathsf{S}\beta
, and
\alpha
to
\alpha
. There is a unique sup-homomorphism
h:(C,\zeta)\rightarrow R
. Each
\beta\leqslant\alpha
has an
h
-preimage
x_{\beta}
, defined recursively via

\begin{array}[]{rcll}x_{\beta}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\rm{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}&\bigvee_{\gamma<\beta}x_{\gamma}&\text{($\beta$ is 0 or a limit)}\\
x_{\mathsf{S}\beta}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\rm{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}&\zeta(x_{\beta})&\text{otherwise}\end{array}

Since
\beta\mapsto x_{\beta}
is a section of
h
, it is injective, contradiction. ∎

Proof of Theorem
4
.
We sketch a proof for the bi-skew case: the other cases follow, including the well-known multicategory case.

We firstly show that a monoid
(x,m,e)
is the biased counterpart of a unique unbiased monoid (dubbed the
“unbiased counterpart”
). To this end, we define the data of the unbiased monoid inductively as follows (for each
n\geq 0
):

\displaystyle m^{\mathsf{LL}}_{0}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\rm{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}e

\displaystyle m^{\mathsf{LL}}_{n+1}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\rm{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}\begin{tabular}[c]{@{}c@{}}\begin{tabular}[b]{>{\centering} b{\@fullwidth} b{0pt}}\parbox[b]{38.52786pt}{\centering$x\begin{tabular}[b]{>{\centering} b{\@fullwidth} b{0pt}}\parbox[b]{32.81259pt}{\centering$~x,\dotsc,x~$\@add@centering}\@add@centering&\\[3.0pt]
\makebox[0.0pt]{}\makebox[0.0pt]{\hbox to33.21pt{\vbox to24.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 16.6063pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.95 0 L -22.7 33.21 L 22.7 33.21 L 3.95 0 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

By induction, each of these is the only possibility, because of the requirements on unbiased monoids. Induction also shows that loosening is respected, in the sense that the loosening of
m^{\mathsf{TL}}_{n+1}
is immediately seen to be
m^{\mathsf{LL}}_{n+1}
, and similarly for the other three possibilities. Preservation of identity is just the definition of
m^{\mathsf{TT}}_{1}
.

To complete the proof, we must show that all composites of these morphisms remain amongst these morphisms. In the first instance, consider a composite:

The left unit law allows us to reduce these to
m^{\mathsf{LL}}_{n}
and
m^{\mathsf{LT}}_{n}
, respectively.

•

Arity 1 (base cases):
f=m^{\mathsf{LT}}_{1}
and
g=m^{\mathsf{LT}}_{1}
or
f=m^{\mathsf{TT}}_{1}
and
g=m^{\mathsf{TT}}_{1}
. These are all (loosened) identities, and they compose to respective identities. For
g
of higher arity,
m^{\mathsf{LT}}_{1}
and
m^{\mathsf{TT}}_{1}
cannot be composed into the first input without loosening first, so this reduces to the previous case.

m^{\mathsf{LT}}_{k+1}

\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\acute{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

x

By the (implicit) induction hypotheses, the upper composites are
m^{\mathsf{LL}}_{k+n}
and
m^{\mathsf{LT}}_{k+n}
, respectively, whence the overall composites are
m^{\mathsf{LL}}_{k+n+1}
and
m^{\mathsf{LT}}_{k+n+1}
by definition of the latter.

•

Inductive step (tight case): the arguments for
f=m^{\mathsf{TL}}_{n+1}
are analogous.

To complete the proof, we use the above as a base case for the situation where
f
is composed into an input
i
further to the right:

f
must be
m^{\mathsf{LL}}_{n}
for some

. Observe that the inductive construction is such that we may decompose
g=m^{\mathsf{TT}}_{k+1}
as follows:

m^{\mathsf{TT}}_{i+1}

x

whence the top factor reduces by the previous case, becoming
m^{\mathsf{LT}}_{k+n-i}
and the overall composite is thus
m^{\mathsf{LT}}_{k+n+1}
. The remaining possibilities for
g
are computed similarly.

Finally, when
f
is in the right-most argument, the composite is of the required form by the reverse of the argument needed to decompose
g
above.

It remains to show that a monoid homomorphism is also a homomorphism between the unbiased counterparts, but this is immediate from the way the unbiased counterpart is constructed. ∎

For a class
E
, the multicategory
\mathbf{SetMat}_{E}
is representable iff
E
is a set. The direction
\Leftarrow
is standard. For
\Rightarrow
, we proceed as follows. If
E
is empty, then it is a set, otherwise
E
has an element
e
. Define matrices
A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\rm{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}(1_{x=e})_{x,y\in E}
and
B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\rm{\tiny def}}}}{{=}}(1_{z=e})_{y,z\in E}
. The list
[A,B]
has a tensor
(C,f)
. We show that the function
E\rightarrow C(e,e)
sending
x\mapsto f_{e,x,e}(*,*)
is injective. Since
C(e,e)
is a set, it follows that
E
is too.

Let
D
be the matrix
(2)_{x,y\in E}
. For any
r,s\!\in\!E
such that
f_{e,r,e}(*,*)=f_{e,s,e}(*,*)
, let
g:A,B\rightarrow D
be the map that at
e,x,e
sends
(*,*)
to
0
if
x=r
and
1
otherwise. We know that
g
factorizes as
f
followed by some
h:C\rightarrow D
, so we have

\displaystyle g_{e,s,e}(*,*)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h_{e,e}(f_{e,s,e}(*,*))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h_{e,e}(f_{e,r,e}(*,*))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{e,r,e}(*,*)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

so
s=r
as required. ∎
